**ChatGPT** *(25.08.2026, 15:50:41)*

Yes — I think this commit is a **real improvement on the HTTP layer**, and it plausibly fixes part of the failure mode you were seeing against an ASP.NET/ASP.NET Core server. I would not expect it to completely solve the NTLM → ASP.NET path yet, though.

The commit makes several important changes:

- It now explicitly handles **persistent HTTP connections** and tries to reuse an existing TCP/TLS connection for the authentication exchange. That matters a lot for NTLM because the exchange is connection-oriented. citeturn688545view0turn856343view0
- It recognizes that a `401` response may contain `Connection: close` and attempts a reconnect instead of blindly writing the Type-3 message onto a dead socket. citeturn688545view0
- The response reader is much more substantial now: it handles `Content-Length`, `Transfer-Encoding: chunked`, and connection-close bodies rather than assuming one `read()` gives you the whole HTTP response. citeturn259528view0turn259528view1
- It added proper parsing of multiple `WWW-Authenticate` headers and supports both `Negotiate` and `NTLM`. citeturn928212view0
- It logs the **raw HTTP response**, including a hex dump and textual representation, which should make the next failure much easier to diagnose. citeturn259528view0

And the existing log strongly suggests that the previous implementation was getting surprisingly far: TLS succeeds, the server returns `401`, the client extracts a **240-byte challenge**, generates a **344-byte Type-3 token**, sends a 624-byte authenticated request — and then ASP.NET returns **400 Bad Request**. citeturn317423view0

That is actually a useful distinction: **the problem is no longer "TLS doesn't work" or "NTLM challenge parsing completely fails."** You're reaching the server with an authentication response and the server is rejecting the resulting HTTP request.

There are, however, two things in this commit that make me suspicious about the remaining 400.

First, this:

```text
Connection: keep-alive
```

is sent on the initial request, while the authenticated request is built with:

```text
Connection: close
```

because `build_request(..., false)` is used for the Type-3 request. citeturn928212view0

That isn't automatically invalid HTTP, but for NTLM specifically the connection semantics are important. The ideal exchange is generally:

```text
request 1  -> 401 + NTLM challenge
request 2  -> Authorization: NTLM Type3
            on the same authenticated connection
```

So I'd be very interested in the **actual raw 401 and raw 400 responses** produced by this commit.

Second, there is a potentially more serious issue in the HTTP body handling: `read_response()` creates a fresh `BufReader` around the underlying stream every time. citeturn259528view0 A `BufReader` can read beyond the exact bytes you logically consumed, and when it is dropped those prefetched bytes are lost. That can become nasty with persistent HTTP/1.1 connections. It may not bite during the simple 401 → Type-3 exchange, but it is absolutely something I'd fix before calling the HTTP implementation robust.

Also, the request generator currently always emits:

```text
HTTP/1.1
Host: <host>
Authorization: ...
User-Agent: rust9x-windows-auth/1.0
Accept: */*
```

and only adds `Content-Length` / `Content-Type: application/json` when there is a body. citeturn928212view0

That's perfectly plausible for a basic ASP.NET API GET, so I don't think you're missing some obvious modern-browser header. In other words, I **wouldn't start spraying random headers at the problem** yet.

### My read of the commit

I'd characterize it as:

**Previous state:**  
`TCP → TLS → 401 → NTLM challenge → Type 3 → 400`, with fairly primitive HTTP framing.

**This commit:**  
`TCP → TLS → correctly framed HTTP response → inspect connection semantics → NTLM challenge → Type 3 → correctly framed HTTP response`.

So yes, it has a good chance of fixing **some** ASP.NET compatibility problems, particularly response parsing and connection lifetime. The fact that the old test already reached `400` means the next run should be much more informative.

The most valuable result from testing this exact commit would be the **raw 400 response** from `http_log.txt`. The commit deliberately added enough logging to tell us whether ASP.NET is saying something like malformed request, invalid authentication context, invalid HTTP version/headers, request-body issue, etc. citeturn259528view0

One particularly interesting detail: the commit itself was tested against `localhost:5001`, and the recorded result still ended in `400 Bad Request` after the Type-3 request. citeturn317423view0 So **the author-level test evidence does not yet show that ASP.NET authentication actually works**. It shows that the transport/framing path has been improved, but the NTLM/ASP.NET interaction is still unresolved at that point.

So I'd say your "hopefully this fixes HTTP handling partially" assessment is exactly right: **yes, partially, and substantially — but the logged 400 says we're not across the finish line yet.**

HttpVersion,\n}\n\n#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]\npub enum HttpVersion {\n    Http1_0,\n    Http1_1,\n    Http2,\n    Unknown(String),\n}\n\n#[derive(Debug, Clone)]\npub struct HttpHeaders {\n    headers: Vec<(String, String)>,\n}\n\nimpl HttpHeaders {\n    pub fn new() -> Self {\n        Self { headers: Vec::new() }\n    }\n\n    pub fn add(&mut self, name: &str, value: &str) {\n        self.headers.push((name.to_lowercase(), value.to_string()));\n    }\n\n    pub fn get(&self, name: &str) -> Option<&String> {\n        let name_lower = name.to_lowercase();\n        self.headers.iter()\n            .find(|(k, _)| k == &name_lower)\n            .map(|(_, v)| v)\n    }\n\n    pub fn get_all(&self, name: &str) -> Vec<&String> {\n        let name_lower = name.to_lowercase();\n        self.headers.iter()\n            .filter(|(k, _)| k == &name_lower)\n            .map(|(_, v)| v)\n            .collect()\n    }\n\n    pub fn contains(&self, name: &str) -> bool {\n        self.get(name).is_some()\n    }\n\n    pub fn content_length(&self) -> Option<usize> {\n        self.get(\"content-length\")\n            .and_then(|v| v.trim().parse::<usize>().ok())\n    }\n\n    pub fn is_chunked(&self) -> bool {\n        self.get(\"transfer-encoding\")\n            .map(|v| v.to_lowercase().contains(\"chunked\"))\n            .unwrap_or(false)\n    }\n\n    pub fn should_close(&self) -> bool {\n        // Check Connection header for explicit close\n        if let Some(conn) = self.get(\"connection\") {\n            if conn.to_lowercase().contains(\"close\") {\n                return true;\n            }\n        }\n        \n        // HTTP/1.0 defaults to close unless keep-alive\n        // HTTP/1.1 defaults to keep-alive unless close\n        false\n    }\n\n    pub fn keep_alive(&self) -> bool {\n        if let Some(conn) = self.get(\"connection\") {\n            return conn.to_lowercase().contains(\"keep-alive\");\n        }\n        false\n    }\n}\n\n/// HTTP client with Windows Authentication support and proper persistent connections\npub struct HttpClient {\n HttpConnection {\n    stream: Box<dyn ReadWrite>,\n    url: ParsedUrl,\n    last_used: std::time::Instant,\n    is_active: bool,\n}\n\n#[cfg(feature = \"network\")]\nimpl HttpConnection {\n    fn new(stream: Box<dyn ReadWrite>, url: ParsedUrl) -> Self {\n        Self {\n            stream,\n            url,\n            last_used: std::time::Instant::now(),\n            is_active: true,\n        }\n    }\n\n    fn is_expired(&self) -> bool {\n        // Connections expire after 2 minutes of inactivity\n        self.last_used.elapsed() > std::time::Duration::from_secs(120)\n    }\n\n    fn close(&mut self) {\n        if self.is_active {\n            // For generic ReadWrite trait, we can't call shutdown directly\n            // The connection will be closed when dropped\n            self.is_active = false;\n        }\n    }\n}\n\nimpl HttpClient {\n    pub fn new() -> Self {\n        Self {\n            #[cfg(feature = \"tls\")]\n            tls_connector: None,\n            #[cfg(feature = \"network\")]\n            connection: None,\n        }\n    }\n\n    #[cfg(feature = \"tls\")]\n    pub fn with_tls(mut self, config: crate::tls::TlsConfig) -> Self {\n        //     // Kept for API compatibility - used for simple response completion checking
    fn is_complete_response(&self, buf: &[u8]) -> bool {
        if let Some(headers_end) = self.find_headers_end(buf) {
            let headers = String::from_utf8_lossy(&buf[..headers_end]);
            
            // Check for Content-Length
            if let Some(cl_line) = headers.lines().find(|l| l.to_lowercase().starts_with(\"content-length:\")) {
                if let Some(len_str) = cl_line.strip_prefix(\"content-length:\")
                    .or_else(|| cl_line.strip_prefix(\"Content-Length:\"))
                {
                    if let Ok(content_len) = len_str.trim().parse::<usize>() {
                        let body_start = headers_end;
                        return buf.len() >= body_start + content_len;
                    }
                }
            }
            
            // If no Content-Length, check for chunked or connection close
            if headers.contains(\"Transfer-Encoding: chunked\") {
                // Simplified check - in production, need proper chunked parsing
                return buf.ends_with(b\"0\\r\
\\r\
\");
            }
            
            // For simple responses without Content-Length, assume complete if we have some data
            return buf.len() > headers_end;
        }
        false
    }
}

**ChatGPT** *(25.08.2026, 15:51:58)*

This log is much more useful than the previous one. The important part is that the **HTTP handling fix did work in one respect, but the remaining failure is now very specifically in the authenticated request**.

The sequence is now:

```text
TLS 1.2 handshake                      SUCCESS
Initial GET                            SUCCESS
Kestrel -> 401 + Negotiate/NTLM       SUCCESS
NTLM challenge parsing                SUCCESS
InitializeSecurityContext(Type 3)     SUCCESS
Type-3 token generated: 344 bytes     SUCCESS
Type-3 request sent on same TLS conn  SUCCESS
Kestrel -> 400 Bad Request             FAIL
```

So I would stop looking at TLS, Schannel, certificate validation, or the initial NTLM challenge. Those are working.

### The strongest clue: this is probably not an authentication failure

Kestrel is specifically returning:

```text
HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request
Content-Length: 0
Connection: close
Server: Kestrel
```

rather than:

```text
401 Unauthorized
WWW-Authenticate: Negotiate ...
```

Kestrel's current documentation says it accepts HTTP/1.0 and HTTP/1.1 and performs strict request parsing/validation; malformed request syntax, headers, Host, request-target, etc. are handled at the Kestrel layer. citeturn442894search3

And your server is explicitly Kestrel:

```text
Server: Kestrel
```

That makes the **actual bytes of the Type-3 HTTP request** the first thing I'd investigate.

Your source constructs it approximately as:

```text
GET /api/v1/auth/windows/windows-login HTTP/1.1
Host: localhost
Authorization: Negotiate <base64 Type3>
User-Agent: rust9x-windows-auth/1.0
Accept: */*
Connection: close

```

The relevant code does exactly that: it adds `Host`, `Authorization`, `User-Agent`, `Accept`, chooses `Connection: close` for the authenticated request, and only adds `Content-Length` when there is a non-empty body. fileciteturn0file0

Nothing there jumps out as obviously illegal HTTP/1.1.

However, there is one thing I would change immediately.

## Don't send `Connection: close` on the Type-3 request yet

Your log says:

```text
[HTTP] Server kept connection alive - reusing for Type 3
```

and then the client sends:

```text
Connection: close
```

That isn't intrinsically invalid, but it's an unnecessary variable in an NTLM exchange.

More importantly, Microsoft explicitly documents that ASP.NET Core Negotiate authentication can retain credentials/state across requests on a connection and warns that the authentication mechanism depends on persistent connection affinity. citeturn442894search0

So for the authenticated request I'd make it:

```http
GET /api/v1/auth/windows/windows-login HTTP/1.1
Host: localhost
Authorization: Negotiate TlRMTVNTUAAD...
User-Agent: rust9x-windows-auth/1.0
Accept: */*
Connection: keep-alive

```

Then let the **server** decide when the connection closes.

Your own commit already has the correct connection-reuse concept; I'd just stop deliberately changing the connection semantics between request 1 and request 2. fileciteturn0file1

### More importantly: log the exact plaintext Type-3 request

Right now you log:

```text
[HTTP] Sending auth request (624 bytes)
```

but not the actual 624 bytes.

That's the missing piece.

I'd add something like:

```rust
log_to_file("----- OUTGOING AUTH HTTP REQUEST -----");
log_to_file(&auth_request);
log_to_file("--------------------------------------");
```

with the token included, because at this point **we need to see exactly what Kestrel receives**.

Even better, log a hex dump too.

The current size is interesting:

```text
Type-3 token: 344 bytes
HTTP request: 624 bytes
```

A Type-3 token of 344 bytes becomes roughly 460 Base64 characters, so the total request size is perfectly plausible. Nothing suggests that the Authorization header is simply too large.

## There is another important point: Kestrel's Negotiate implementation

Microsoft explicitly says something slightly unusual about Kestrel:

> Kestrel only sends `WWW-Authenticate: Negotiate`, and NTLM must be carried using the `Negotiate` prefix. citeturn442894search0

Your client is doing exactly this:

```text
Authorization: Negotiate <NTLM Type 3>
```

So **that part is correct**. Do not change it to:

```text
Authorization: NTLM <token>
```

That would actually be wrong for Kestrel. citeturn442894search0

And your decoded server challenge starts with:

```text
TlRMTVNTUAAC
```

which is NTLMSSP Type 2, so Kestrel is indeed selecting NTLM underneath Negotiate. Microsoft documents that exact mechanism. citeturn442894search0

## The really interesting possibility

I think there are now two main possibilities.

### 1. Kestrel's HTTP parser is rejecting something in the generated request

This would explain the immediate 400 and zero-length body.

Likely suspects:

- exact CRLF termination
- malformed header syntax
- hidden/non-printable bytes in the generated request
- invalid `Host`
- something about the Authorization header serialization
- a request-target/path construction bug

Your source appears to construct the request manually rather than through a standard HTTP serializer. fileciteturn0file0

That means a single subtle formatting mistake can produce exactly this kind of result.

### 2. The Negotiate middleware itself is returning 400

This is also possible.

There are real cases where ASP.NET Core/Negotiate setups produce a 400 around Windows authentication, and the useful diagnostic distinction is whether Kestrel logs a request parser error or the request reaches the authentication middleware first. citeturn998199search3turn998199search0

So the single most useful server-side test now is to turn on:

```json
{
  "Logging": {
    "LogLevel": {
      "Microsoft.AspNetCore.Server.Kestrel": "Debug",
      "Microsoft.AspNetCore.Server.Kestrel.BadRequests": "Debug",
      "Microsoft.AspNetCore.Authentication.Negotiate": "Debug"
    }
  }
}
```

The `Kestrel.BadRequests` category is especially useful for determining **why Kestrel decided the request was malformed**. citeturn998199search3

Then run exactly one request.

If the server says something like:

```text
Invalid request line
Invalid header
Invalid Host header
Invalid request target
```

then we know the Rust HTTP builder is the problem.

If instead it says something along the lines of:

```text
Request starting HTTP/1.1 GET ...
Negotiate authentication failed
```

then the HTTP request itself was accepted and we're down to NTLM/Negotiate compatibility.

---

### One thing I would *not* touch

The SSPI part looks healthy.

You have:

```text
AcquireCredentialsHandle -> SUCCESS
InitializeSecurityContext -> SUCCESS
InitializeSecurityContext (challenge) -> SUCCESS
Authenticate token generated (344 bytes)
```

And the NTLM challenge is a legitimate Type-2 message containing target information. The fact that the Type-3 token is generated successfully by Windows SSPI is a very strong sign that we shouldn't start rewriting NTLM ourselves.

Also, the fact that the **same connection is successfully reused** after the 401 is good. Your commit's connection handling is demonstrably doing what it was intended to do. fileciteturn0file1

### My current diagnosis

I'd rank it:

**Most likely:** malformed/manual HTTP request serialization on the Type-3 request.

**Second:** Kestrel's Negotiate middleware doesn't like something about this particular NTLM Type-3 exchange and is converting it to 400.

**Much less likely:** TLS or connection reuse.

And the next change I'd make is very small:

```rust
let auth_request =
    self.build_request(url, method, &body,
                       &format!("Negotiate {}", auth_b64),
                       true); // keep-alive, not false
```

followed by logging the **exact plaintext `auth_request`** before encryption.

That should tell us very quickly whether commit `8d433899` has basically finished the HTTP transport work and the remaining problem is inside Kestrel/Negotiate, or whether the hand-built request itself still has a defect.

TicketSalesApp.AdminServer.Controllers.v1\n{\n    /// <summary>\n    /// Handles Windows Authentication and account linking operations\n    /// </summary>\n    [ApiController]\n    [Route(\"api/v1/auth/windows\")]\n    public class WindowsAuthController : ControllerBase\n    {\n        private readonly TicketSalesApp.AdminServer.Services.Interfaces.IWindowsAuthBusinessService _windowsAuthBusinessService;\n        private readonly ILogger<WindowsAuthController> _logger;\n\n        public WindowsAuthController(\n            TicketSalesApp.AdminServer.Services.Interfaces.IWindowsAuthBusinessService windowsAuthBusinessService,\n            ILogger<WindowsAuthController> logger)\n        {\n            _windowsAuthBusinessService = windowsAuthBusinessService;\n            _logger = logger;\n        }\n\n        /// <summary>\n        /// Windows authentication login endpoint\n        /// </summary>\n        [Route(\"windows-login\")]\n        [Authorize(AuthenticationSchemes = \"Windows\")]\n        [HttpGet]\n        public async Task<IActionResult> WindowsLogin()\n        {\n            Console.WriteLine(\"[WindowsLogin] Starting Windows authentication flow\");\n            try\n            {\n                Console.WriteLine(\"[WindowsLogin] Checking Windows authentication status\");\n                if (!(User.Identity is WindowsIdentity wi) || !wi.IsAuthenticated)\n                {\n                    Console.WriteLine(\"[WindowsLogin] User not authenticated, triggering Windows auth challenge\");\n                    return Challenge(new AuthenticationProperties(),\n                        NegotiateDefaults.AuthenticationScheme);\n                }\n\n                var windowsUsername = wi.Name;\n                Console.WriteLine($\"[WindowsLogin] Windows user authenticated: {windowsUsername}\");\n\n                // Use business service for authentication\n                var (success, token, user, message) = await _windowsAuthBusinessService.AuthenticateWindowsUserAsync(windowsUsername);\n\n                if (!success)\n                {\n                    Console.WriteLine($\"[WindowsLogin] Authentication failed: {message}\");\n                    \n                    // Check if it's a security issue (blank password)\n                    if (message.Contains(\"not securely configured\"))\n                    {\n                        return StatusCode(418, new\n                        {\n                            message = message,\n                            codedtest = 418,\n                            secondaryText = \"I'm a teapot—not really, but your account looks like one.\"\n                        });\n                    }\n                    \n                    return BadRequest(new { message });\n                }\n\n                Console.WriteLine($\"[WindowsLogin] Authentication successful for user: {user.Login}\");\n                \n                // Extract Windows authentication protocol information\n                var authInfo = new\n                {\n                    AuthenticationType = wi.AuthenticationType,\n                    IsAuthenticated = wi.IsAuthenticated,\n                    IsGuest = wi.IsGuest,\n                    IsSystem = wi.IsSystem,\n                    IsAnonymous = wi.IsAnonymous,\n                    UserSid = wi.User?.ToString(),\n                    OwnerSid = wi.Owner?.ToString(),\n                    ImpersonationLevel = wi.ImpersonationLevel.ToString(),\n                    Token = wi.Token.ToString(),\n                    Groups = wi.Groups?.Select(g => g.ToString()).ToArray()\n                };\n\n                // Extract HTTP authentication headers\n                var authHeaders = new\n                {\n                    Authorization = HttpContext.Request.Headers[\"Authorization\"].ToString(),\n                    WwwAuthenticate = HttpContext.Request.Headers[\"Www-Authenticate\"].ToString()\n                };\n\n                var response = new\n                {\n                    token,\n                    user = new\n                    {\n                        user.UserId,\n                        user.Login,\n                        user.Email,\n                        user.Role,\n                        IsWindowsAuth = true,\n                        DoesWindowsAccountNeedLinking = user.DoesWindowsAccountNeedLinking\n                    },\n                    windowsAuth = new\n                    {\n                        protocol = authInfo.AuthenticationType,\n                        isNtlm = authInfo.AuthenticationType?.Equals(\"NTLM\", StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase) ?? false,\n                        isNegotiate = authInfo.AuthenticationType?.Equals(\"Negotiate\", StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase) ?? false,\n                        isKerberos = authInfo.AuthenticationType?.Equals(\"Kerberos\", StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase) ?? false,\n                        authenticationDetails = authInfo,\n                        httpHeaders = authHeaders\n                    }\n                };\n\n                Console.WriteLine($\"[WindowsLogin] Returning successful authentication response with protocol info: {authInfo.AuthenticationType}\");\n                return Ok(response);\n            }\n            catch (Exception ex)\n            {\n                Console.WriteLine($\"[ERROR] Unexpected         {\n                    message = \"An internal server error occurred during authentication.\"\n                });\n            }\n        }\n\n        /// <summary>\n        /// Check Windows account link status\n        /// </summary>\n        [Route(\"check-windows-link-status\")]\n        [Authorize]\n        [HttpGet]\n        public async Task<IActionResult> CheckWindowsLinkStatus()\n IConfiguration _configuration;\n        private readonly IWebHostEnvironment _environment;\n\n        public Startup(IConfiguration configuration, IWebHostEnvironment environment)\n        {\n            _configuration = configuration;\n            _environment = environment;\n\n            // Create a temporary logger factory for startup\n            var loggerFactory = LoggerFactory.Create(builder =>\n            {\n                builder.AddConsole();\n                builder.AddDebug();\n\n                if (environment.IsDevelopment())\n                {\n                    builder.SetMinimumLevel(LogLevel.Debug);\n                }\n                else\n                {\n                    builder.SetMinimumLevel(LogLevel.Information);\n                }\n            });\n\n            _logger = loggerFactory.CreateLogger<Startup>();\n        }\n\n        public IConfiguration Configuration => _configuration;\n        public IWebHostEnvironment Environment => _environment;\n\n        public void ConfigureServices(IServiceCollection services)\n        {\n            // Configure logging first\n            services.AddLogging(builder =>\n            {\n                builder.ClearProviders();\n                builder.AddConsole();\n                builder.AddDebug();\n                if (Environment.IsDevelopment())\n                {\n                    builder.SetMinimumLevel(LogLevel.Debug);\n                }\n                else\n                {\n                    builder.SetMinimumLevel(LogLevel.Information);\n                }\n            });\n\n            services.AddControllers(options =>\n            {\n                options.RespectBrowserAcceptHeader = true;\n            })\n            .AddJsonOptions(options =>\n            {\n                options.JsonSerializerOptions.ReferenceHandler = System.Text.Json.Serialization.ReferenceHandler.Preserve;\n            });\n\n            try\n            {\n                // Configure primary database (SQL) - keep existing approach\n                var provider = Configuration.GetValue<string>(\"DatabaseProvider\", \"SQLite\");\n                var dbPath = Path.Combine(AppContext.BaseDirectory, \"ticketsales.db\");\n\n                if (provider == \"SQLite\")\n                {\n                    // Ensure the directory exists\n                    var directory = Path.GetDirectoryName(dbPath);\n                    if (!Directory.Exists(directory))\n                    {\n                        Directory.CreateDirectory(directory!);\n                    }\n\n                    services.AddDbContext<AppDbContext>(options =>\n                    {\n                        options.UseSqlite($\"Data Source={dbPath}\");\n                        // Enable detailed errors in development\n                        if (Environment.IsDevelopment())\n                        {\n                            options.EnableDetailedErrors();\n                            options.EnableSensitiveDataLogging();\n                        }\n                    }, contextLifetime: ServiceLifetime.Scoped,\n                       optionsLifetime: ServiceLifetime.Singleton);\n\n                    services.AddScoped(sp =>\n                        new AppDbContext(sp.GetRequiredService<DbContextOptions<AppDbContext>>(), \"SQLite\"));\n                }\n                else\n                {\n                    services.AddDbContext<AppDbContext>(options =>\n                    {\n                        options.UseSqlServer(Configuration.GetConnectionString(\"DefaultConnection\"),\n                            b => b.MigrationsAssembly(\"TicketSalesApp.Core\"));\n                        // Enable detailed errors in development\n                        if (Environment.IsDevelopment())\n                        {\n                            options.EnableDetailedErrors();\n                            options.EnableSensitiveDataLogging();\n                        }\n                    }, contextLifetime: ServiceLifetime.Scoped,\n                       optionsLifetime: ServiceLifetime.Singleton);\n\n                    services.AddScoped(sp =>\n                        new AppDbContext(sp.GetRequiredService<DbContextOptions<AppDbContext>>(), \"SQLServer\"));\n                }\n\n                // Add enhanced database services (MongoDB, Redis, Repository pattern)\n                services.AddDatabaseServices(Configuration);\n                \n                _logger.LogInformation(\"Enhanced database architecture configured successfully with primary SQL database and MongoDB/Redis support\");\n            }\n            catch (Exception ex)\n            {\n                _logger.LogCritical(ex, \"Failed to configure enhanced database architecture. Application startup will be aborted.\");\n                throw;\n            }\n\n            // Configure CORS\n            services.AddCors(options =>\n            {\n                options.AddPolicy(\"AllowAll\", builder =>\n                {\n                    builder\n                        .AllowAnyOrigin()\n                        .AllowAnyMethod()\n                        .AllowAnyHeader()\n                        .WithExposedHeaders(\"Content-Disposition\", \"Authorization\");\n                });\n\n                // Specific CORS policy for SignalR\n                options.AddPolicy(\"SignalRCors\", builder =>\n                {\n                    builder\n                        .WithOrigins(\"http://localhost:3000\", \"https://localhost:3001\", \"http://localhost:5000\", \"https://localhost:5001\")\n                        .AllowAnyMethod()\n                        .AllowAnyHeader()\n                        .AllowCredentials(); // Required for SignalR\n                });\n            });\n\n            // Configure Authentication\n            try\n            {\n                // Configure JWT Authentication\n                var jwtSettings = Configuration.GetSection(\"JwtSettings\");\n                var key = Encoding.ASCII.GetBytes(jwtSettings[\"Secret\"] ??\n                    throw new InvalidOperationException(\"JWT Secret is not configured in appsettings.json\"));\n\n                // Configure Windows Authentication\n                services.AddAuthentication(options =>\n                {\n                    // Set default schemes\n                    options.DefaultAuthenticateScheme = NegotiateDefaults.AuthenticationScheme;\n                    options.DefaultChallengeScheme = NegotiateDefaults.AuthenticationScheme;\n                    options.DefaultScheme = JwtBearerDefaults.AuthenticationScheme;\n                })\n                .AddJwtBearer(JwtBearerDefaults.AuthenticationScheme, options =>\n                {\n                    options.RequireHttpsMetadata = !Environment.IsDevelopment();\n                    options.SaveToken = true;\n                    options.TokenValidationParameters = new TokenValidationParameters\n                    {\n                        ValidateIssuerSigningKey = true,\n                        IssuerSigningKey = new SymmetricSecurityKey(key),\n                        ValidateIssuer = false,\n                        ValidateAudience = false,\n                        ClockSkew = TimeSpan.Zero,\n                        RoleClaimType = \"role\"\n                    };\n                })\n                // Add Windows Authentication (Negotiate)\n                .AddNegotiate(\"Windows\", options =>\n                {\n                    // Require either Kerberos or NTLM with enhanced security\n                    options.PersistKerberosCredentials=false;\n                    options.PersistNtlmCredentials=false;\n                });\n\n                // Add our custom authorization handlers\n                services.AddSingleton<IAuthorizationHandler, WindowsAuthSecurityHandler>();\n                services.AddScoped<IAuthorizationHandler, DatabaseRoleAuthorizationHandler>();\n                services.AddHttpContextAccessor();\n\n                // Add authorization policies\n                services.AddAuthorization(options =>\n                {\n                    // Configure database-backed authorization policies\n                    TicketSalesApp.AdminServer.Configuration.AuthorizationPolicies.ConfigurePolicies(options);\n                        \n                    // Windows Authentication policy with enhanced security\n                    options.AddPolicy(\"WindowsAuth\", policy =>\n                    {\n                        policy.RequireAuthenticatedUser()\n                              .AddAuthenticationSchemes(\"Windows\")\n                              .AddRequirements(new WindowsAuthSecurityRequirement());\n                    });\n\n                    // Combined policy that allows either JWT or Windows auth\n                    options.DefaultPolicy = new AuthorizationPolicyBuilder()\n                        .AddAuthenticationSchemes(JwtBearerDefaults.AuthenticationScheme, \"Windows\")\n                        .RequireAuthenticatedUser()\n                        .Build();\n                });\n            }\n            catch (Exception ex)\n            {\n                _logger.LogCritical(ex, \"Failed to configure authentication. Application startup will be aborted.\");\n                throw;\n            }\n\n            services.AddSerilog();\n\n            // Register Services\n            services.AddScoped<IAuthenticationService, AuthenticationService>();\n            services.AddScoped<ITicketSalesService, TicketSalesService>();\n            services.AddScoped<IDataService, DataService>();\n            \n            // Register Business Services\n            services.AddScoped<TicketSalesApp.AdminServer.Services.Interfaces.IAuthenticationBusinessService, TicketSalesApp.AdminServer.Services.AuthenticationBusinessService>();\n            services.AddScoped<TicketSalesApp.AdminServer.Services.Interfaces.IWindowsAuthBusinessService, TicketSalesApp.AdminServer.Services.WindowsAuthBusinessService>();\n            \n            // Add memory cache and distributed cache\n            services.AddMemoryCache();\n            services.AddDistributedMemoryCache(); // Required for Fido2 library\n            \n            // Configure Redis for session state and distributed caching\n            ConfigureRedisServices(services);\n            \n            // Configure WebAuthn (FIDO2) services\n            ConfigureWebAuthn(services);\n            \n            services.AddHttpContextAccessor();\n            // Rate Limiting\n            services.Configure<RateLimitOptions>(Configuration.GetSection(RateLimitOptions.RateLimit));\n            var rateLimitOptions = new RateLimitOptions();\n            Configuration.GetSection(RateLimitOptions.RateLimit).Bind(rateLimitOptions);\n\n            services.AddRateLimiter(options =>\n            {\n                options.AddFixedWindowLimiter(\"fixed\", opt =>\n                {\n                    opt.PermitLimit = rateLimitOptions.PermitLimit;\n                    opt.Window = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(rateLimitOptions.Window);\n                    opt.QueueLimit = rateLimitOptions.QueueLimit;\n                });\n\n                options.AddSlidingWindowLimiter(\"sliding\", opt =>\n                {\n                    opt.PermitLimit = rateLimitOptions.PermitLimit;\n                    opt.Window = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(rateLimitOptions.Window);\n                    opt.SegmentsPerWindow = rateLimitOptions.SegmentsPerWindow;\n                    opt.QueueLimit = rateLimitOptions.QueueLimit;\n                });\n\n                options.AddTokenBucketLimiter(\"token\", opt =>\n                {\n                    opt.TokenLimit = rateLimitOptions.TokenLimit;\n                    opt.QueueLimit = rateLimitOptions.QueueLimit;\n                    opt.TokensPerPeriod = rateLimitOptions.TokensPerPeriod;\n                    opt.ReplenishmentPeriod = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(rateLimitOptions.ReplenishmentPeriod);\n                });\n\n                options.AddConcurrencyLimiter(\"concurrency\", opt =>\n                {\n                    opt.PermitLimit = rateLimitOptions.ConcurrencyLimit;\n                    opt.QueueLimit = rateLimitOptions.QueueLimit;\n                });\n\n                options.OnRejected = async (context, token) =>\n                {\n                    if (context.Lease.TryGetMetadata(MetadataName.RetryAfter, out var retryAfter))\n                    {\n [L2]   "content": "using Microsoft.AspNetCore.Authorization;\nusing Microsoft.AspNetCore.Http;\nusing Microsoft.Extensions.Logging;\nusing System.DirectoryServices.AccountManagement;\nusing System.Runtime.InteropServices;\nusing System.Security.Claims;\nusing System.Security.Principal;\nusing System.Threading.Tasks;\n\nnamespace TicketSalesApp.AdminServer.Security\n{\n    public class WindowsAuthSecurityRequirement : IAuthorizationRequirement { }\n\n    public class WindowsAuthSecurityHandler : AuthorizationHandler<WindowsAuthSecurityRequirement>\n    {\n        private readonly ILogger<WindowsAuthSecurityHandler> _logger;\n        private readonly IHttpContextAccessor _httpContextAccessor;\n\n        public WindowsAuthSecurityHandler(\n            ILogger<WindowsAuthSecurityHandler> logger,\n            IHttpContextAccessor httpContextAccessor)\n        {\n            _logger = logger;\n            _httpContextAccessor = httpContextAccessor;\n        }\n\n        protected override async Task HandleRequirementAsync(\n            AuthorizationHandlerContext context,\n            WindowsAuthSecurityRequirement requirement)\n        {\n            var httpContext = _httpContextAccessor.HttpContext;\n            if (httpContext == null)\n            {\n                _logger.LogWarning(\"HTTP context is not available\");\n                return;\n            }\n\n            var windowsIdentity = httpContext.User.Identity as WindowsIdentity;\n            if (windowsIdentity == null || !windowsIdentity.IsAuthenticated)\n            {\n                _logger.LogWarning(\"User is not authenticated with Windows Authentication\");\n                return;\n            }\n\n            // Check if the authentication was done with a password or Windows Hello\n            var authType = windowsIdentity.AuthenticationType?.ToUpperInvariant() ?? string.Empty;\n            \n            // Authentication types that indicate password or Windows Hello\n            var secureAuthTypes = new[] { \"NEGOTIATE\", \"KERBEROS\", \"NTLM\", \"PASSPORT\" };\n            \n            if (secureAuthTypes.Contains(authType))\n            {\n                // Additional check for blank passwords if using NTLM\n                if (authType == \"NTLM\" && HasBlankPassword(windowsIdentity.Name))\n                {\n                    _logger.LogWarning(\"Windows Authentication failed: User has a blank password which is not allowed due to security purposes\");\n                    return;\n                }\n                \n                context.Succeed(requirement);\n                return;\n            }\n\n            _logger.LogWarning(\"Windows Authentication failed: Unsecure authentication method detected: {AuthType}\", authType);\n        }\n\n        private bool HasBlankPassword(string username)\n        {\n            try\n            {\n                using (var context = new PrincipalContext(ContextType.Domain))\n                using (var user = UserPrincipal.FindByIdentity(context, IdentityType.SamAccountName, username))\n                {\n                    if (user == null)\n                    {\n                        _logger.LogWarning(\"Could not find user in Active Directory: {Username}\", username);\n                        return false;\n                    }\n\n                    // Try to authenticate with a blank password\n                    return context.ValidateCredentials(user.SamAccountName, \"\", ContextOptions.Negotiate);\n                }\n            }\n            catch (Exception ex)\n            {\n                _logger.LogError(ex, \"Error checking for blank password for user: {Username}\", username);\n                return false; // Fail securely by not allowing access\n            }\n        }\n    }\n}\n",

**ChatGPT** *(25.08.2026, 15:59:47)*

Ah — with the actual server code, the picture is much clearer.

The ASP.NET app is indeed the intended test target, and the endpoint is exactly:

`GET /api/v1/auth/windows/windows-login`

with:

```csharp
[Authorize(AuthenticationSchemes = "Windows")]
[HttpGet]
```

so the Rust client's target URL is correct. fileciteturn5file0

More importantly, the server config confirms this is **ASP.NET Core Negotiate authentication on Kestrel**, not IIS Windows Authentication. It registers:

```csharp
.AddNegotiate("Windows", options =>
{
    options.PersistKerberosCredentials = false;
    options.PersistNtlmCredentials = false;
});
```

and then uses the `"Windows"` scheme on the endpoint. fileciteturn8file0

That changes my diagnosis quite a bit.

### The NTLM exchange is reaching the correct authentication handler

Your server sends:

```text
401 Unauthorized
WWW-Authenticate: Negotiate TlRMTVNTUAAC...
```

which is exactly what I'd expect from this setup.

Your Rust client then produces a Type-3 NTLM token and sends:

```text
Authorization: Negotiate <Type3>
```

That's also the correct scheme for this ASP.NET Core Negotiate configuration.

The interesting part is that **the controller itself apparently never gets reached**.

The controller begins with:

```csharp
Console.WriteLine("[WindowsLogin] Starting Windows authentication flow");
```

but your client gets an immediate:

```text
HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request
Content-Length: 0
Connection: close
Server: Kestrel
```

There is no JSON body from `WindowsLogin()`, and the controller's own `BadRequest(new { message })` path would produce a JSON response rather than an empty response.

So this 400 is almost certainly happening **before the controller executes**.

### And now I see an important possibility

The server explicitly configures:

```csharp
.AddNegotiate("Windows", options =>
{
    options.PersistKerberosCredentials = false;
    options.PersistNtlmCredentials = false;
});
```

That means the ASP.NET Core Negotiate handler is handling the NTLM security context itself. fileciteturn8file0

Meanwhile, your client is doing the NTLM conversation using:

```text
AcquireCredentialsHandle
InitializeSecurityContext
InitializeSecurityContext(challenge)
```

and reusing the **same TLS connection**, which is correct for NTLM.

But there is a subtle detail in your server's Negotiate configuration: `PersistNtlmCredentials = false` means the server does **not persist the NTLM credentials beyond the connection/request lifecycle**. This makes connection identity particularly important.

Your current client does keep the connection, but then constructs the authenticated request as:

```text
Connection: close
```

because the second call is:

```rust
build_request(..., false)
```

Your source confirms this directly. fileciteturn0file1

I therefore agree with the earlier suspicion, but now we have a concrete reason to try it:

**the Type-3 request should absolutely be sent with `Connection: keep-alive` while debugging this.**

Not because `Connection: close` is generally illegal, but because we're testing a connection-bound Negotiate/NTLM state machine.

### There is another server-side factor: the custom authorization handler

The project also has a custom:

```text
WindowsAuthSecurityHandler
```

which checks that the resulting `WindowsIdentity` is authenticated and that its authentication type is one of:

```text
NEGOTIATE
KERBEROS
NTLM
PASSPORT
```

fileciteturn9file0

But this handler is only used by the custom `"WindowsAuth"` authorization policy. The `windows-login` endpoint uses `[Authorize(AuthenticationSchemes = "Windows")]`, not the custom policy. fileciteturn5file0

So **that handler isn't what is generating your current 400**.

### The endpoint itself is actually extremely simple

After successful Negotiate authentication, it does:

```text
WindowsIdentity wi = User.Identity
wi.IsAuthenticated
wi.Name
WindowsAuthBusinessService.AuthenticateWindowsUserAsync(...)
```

and then returns JSON containing the authentication details. fileciteturn5file0

Therefore the test we're trying to achieve is quite literally:

```text
Rust client
   │
   │ GET /api/v1/auth/windows/windows-login
   ▼
Kestrel
   │
   ├── 401 + Negotiate/NTLM Type 2
   │
   │ GET + Authorization: Negotiate Type 3
   ▼
ASP.NET Core Negotiate handler
   │
   ▼
WindowsIdentity
   │
   ▼
WindowsLogin()
   │
   ▼
200 JSON
```

We're currently stuck exactly between the Negotiate handler and `WindowsLogin()`.

### One thing I now find especially suspicious: `localhost`

The Type-2 challenge from the server contains target information referring to:

```text
DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

while your client explicitly initializes SSPI with:

```text
TargetName: HTTP/localhost
```

That isn't necessarily wrong, but it is worth testing because the server is actually advertising its machine name in the NTLM target information.

The challenge contains:

```text
DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

and your SSPI target is:

```text
HTTP/localhost
```

So there are two names involved:

```text
HTTP/localhost
DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

For a normal Windows client accessing:

```text
https://localhost:5001
```

Windows' own HTTP authentication stack may handle this differently than your explicit `InitializeSecurityContext("HTTP/localhost")` call.

I would **not** change this blindly yet, because the Type-3 token is being generated successfully and we need to determine whether the problem is HTTP framing or SSPI target naming.

### My revised priority order

1. **Send Type-3 with `Connection: keep-alive`**, not `close`.
2. **Log the exact decrypted HTTP request bytes before TLS encryption.**
3. Enable server-side debug logging for:
   ```text
   Microsoft.AspNetCore.Authentication.Negotiate
   Microsoft.AspNetCore.Server.Kestrel
   ```
4. Check whether `[WindowsLogin] Starting Windows authentication flow` appears at all.
5. Only then investigate `HTTP/localhost` vs `HTTP/DESKTOP-I4OV31Q`.

The source confirms the Rust side is currently deliberately switching the Type-3 request to `Connection: close`. fileciteturn0file1 The server, meanwhile, is explicitly running the ASP.NET Core `"Windows"` Negotiate handler under Kestrel. fileciteturn8file0

So at this point I'd **not call the commit a failure**. The commit fixed the obvious HTTP-response/connection handling enough that we're now getting a clean, repeatable 401 → Type-3 → 400 sequence. The remaining bug is much narrower.

And yes: now that I have the actual `bru-year1coursework` source, we can reason about this as **Rust9x Windows authentication client ↔ this specific ASP.NET Core/Kestrel Negotiate server**, rather than generic ASP.NET behavior.

This test rules out the `Connection: close` theory. The authenticated request is now a clean 629-byte HTTP/1.1 request with `keep-alive`, yet Kestrel still returns bare 400. The key is now the NTLM token/server-side Negotiate behavior, not HTTP persistence.

**ChatGPT** *(25.08.2026, 16:01:35)*

Yep — this new log changes the diagnosis substantially.

The `keep-alive` test is clean, and the outgoing request itself looks syntactically sane:

```text
GET /api/v1/auth/windows/windows-login HTTP/1.1
Host: localhost
Authorization: Negotiate TlRMTVNTUAAD...
User-Agent: rust9x-windows-auth/1.0
Accept: */*
Connection: keep-alive
```

There is no obvious HTTP/1.1 parser violation here. Kestrel's documented parser rejects things such as missing/invalid `Host`, malformed header syntax, invalid request targets, etc.; your request does not show any of those problems. citeturn719913search2

And the really important clue is this:

**Kestrel returning bare `400 Bad Request` after a Type-3 Negotiate token is a known symptom of the ASP.NET Core `NegotiateHandler` rejecting the authentication token.**

There is an almost exact reproduction of this behavior: Kestrel logs `NegotiateHandler[11] Negotiate error code: ClientError`, followed by `NegotiateHandler[10] The users authentication request was invalid`, with a Win32 error `0x80090308` (`SEC_E_INVALID_TOKEN`), and then the HTTP request finishes as `400 0`. citeturn427132search0

That is *extremely* close to what you're seeing.

So I think we're now at:

```text
Rust HTTP                 OK
TLS                       OK
401 challenge              OK
NTLM Type-2 parsing        OK
Type-3 generation          OK
Authorization header       OK
Connection persistence     OK
             ↓
ASP.NET Core NegotiateHandler
             ↓
          rejects Type 3
             ↓
            400
```

### This also explains why the controller is never reached

Your `WindowsLogin()` controller isn't the component producing the 400.

The endpoint has:

```csharp
[Authorize(AuthenticationSchemes = "Windows")]
[HttpGet]
```

so the Negotiate authentication handler must successfully establish the `WindowsIdentity` before the action executes. fileciteturn5file0

And the server configuration explicitly registers:

```csharp
.AddNegotiate("Windows", options =>
{
    options.PersistKerberosCredentials = false;
    options.PersistNtlmCredentials = false;
});
```

fileciteturn8file0

Thus a bad Type-3 token gets consumed by `NegotiateHandler` before your `WindowsLogin()` code has a chance to run.

### The next thing to inspect is **not HTTP**

It's the actual NTLM Type-3 token.

Your token begins correctly:

```text
TlRMTVNTUAAD
```

which is NTLMSSP Type 3.

But "Windows `InitializeSecurityContext()` returned success" does **not** mean that the server will accept that token. It means Microsoft's local SSPI provider successfully constructed a client-side authentication message.

The server has its own SSPI state and validates that message independently.

And there are several things that can make the resulting token unacceptable:

- wrong NTLM target/service name
- mismatched authentication context
- incorrect NTLM negotiate flags
- credential/domain representation
- MIC/channel-binding-related data
- connection/security-context association
- differences in how the server's `NegotiateStream`/SSPI side expects the exchange to be completed

### `HTTP/localhost` is now much more suspicious

Your client explicitly tells SSPI:

```text
TargetName: HTTP/localhost
```

for both Type 1 and Type 3.

But the NTLM Type-2 challenge contains:

```text
DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

in the target information.

Microsoft's Kestrel documentation says that Kestrel's `WWW-Authenticate: Negotiate` can contain NTLM, and the NTLM exchange is performed through the Negotiate mechanism. citeturn719913search0

The fact that the server's challenge is explicitly identifying the machine while the client's SSPI target is `HTTP/localhost` makes this worth testing. I don't want to claim that this is definitely the bug — NTLM can legitimately authenticate against aliases depending on SPNs/configuration — but it is now one of the strongest hypotheses.

### There is an even better experiment

Use the **actual server hostname** instead of `localhost` for the whole request.

For example, from your challenge:

```text
DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

try:

```text
https://DESKTOP-I4OV31Q:5001/api/v1/auth/windows/windows-login
```

and therefore:

```text
Host: DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
TargetName: HTTP/DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
SNI: DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

That gives every layer the same service identity.

Right now you effectively have:

```text
TCP endpoint       localhost:5001
HTTP Host           localhost
TLS SNI             localhost
NTLM target SPN     HTTP/localhost
NTLM server target  DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

That's an unnecessarily weird identity combination for a Windows authentication test.

### And the server itself gives us a perfect diagnostic

Turn on:

```json
{
  "Logging": {
    "LogLevel": {
      "Microsoft.AspNetCore.Authentication.Negotiate": "Debug",
      "Microsoft.AspNetCore.Server.Kestrel": "Debug",
      "Microsoft.AspNetCore.Server.Kestrel.BadRequests": "Debug"
    }
  }
}
```

There is precedent for the exact `400` you're getting being logged as:

```text
Microsoft.AspNetCore.Authentication.Negotiate.NegotiateHandler
Negotiate error code: ClientError

The users authentication request was invalid.

Win32Exception (0x80090308)
```

citeturn427132search0

If your server prints **`0x80090308 / SEC_E_INVALID_TOKEN`**, then we have essentially proven:

> the HTTP layer is no longer the problem; the server rejects the NTLM Type-3 message.

And that's excellent news because it drastically reduces the search space.

### One more thing I noticed in your Type-3

Your client is using:

```text
ContextRequirements: CONNECTION | ALLOCATE_MEMORY
```

for the client SSPI context.

That may be important. We should compare the exact SSPI flags used by a known-good Windows client and the flags resulting in your Type-3. The byte-level token can tell us whether the NTLM negotiation flags and target information are what Kestrel/Windows expects.

At this point, I would **not make more arbitrary HTTP changes**. The request is now valid-looking and persistent, and we have a known Kestrel behavior where an invalid Negotiate token manifests exactly as a bare 400. citeturn427132search0turn719913search2

The best next test is therefore:

**Run the same Rust client with server-side `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Authentication.Negotiate` debug logging enabled and see the actual Negotiate error code.**

That one line should tell us whether we're dealing with `SEC_E_INVALID_TOKEN`, a target/SPN issue, or something else.

**ChatGPT** *(25.08.2026, 16:05:07)*

This finally gives us the decisive server-side evidence.

The important lines are:

```text
Request matched endpoint '...WindowsLogin'
Incomplete Negotiate handshake, sending an additional 401 Negotiate challenge.
```

on the first request, followed by:

```text
Negotiate error code: ClientError.
The users authentication request was invalid.
Microsoft.AspNetCore.Authentication.AuthenticationFailureException: InvalidToken
```

on the Type-3 request.

So **the HTTP request is absolutely reaching Kestrel and routing correctly**. The controller is not executing because the ASP.NET Core Negotiate handler rejects the NTLM Type-3 token. The bare 400 is produced from that authentication failure, not by the HTTP parser.

That means the situation is now:

```text
Rust client
    |
    | TLS 1.2
    v
Kestrel
    |
    | HTTP parsed successfully
    | route matched successfully
    v
NegotiateHandler
    |
    | Type 2 challenge -> Rust
    |
    | Type 3 <- Rust
    |
    X InvalidToken / ClientError
    |
    v
HTTP 400
```

That is a very solid conclusion from your log.

### The Redis errors are unrelated

The gigantic wall of:

```text
StackExchange.Redis.RedisConnectionException
localhost:6379
UnableToConnect
```

is a separate problem in the test server.

It happens while `SessionMiddleware.CommitAsync()` is trying to use Redis, and it causes the requests to take ~5–30 seconds to finish. But the authentication failure is independently reported as:

```text
Negotiate error code: ClientError
AuthenticationFailureException: InvalidToken
```

So Redis is making the test environment noisy and slow, but **it is not why Negotiate rejects the Type-3 token**.

In fact, the sequence is quite revealing:

```text
16:00:18  first request -> incomplete handshake -> 401
16:00:32  second request -> InvalidToken
16:00:37  final HTTP 400
```

The ~6 seconds between the InvalidToken message and request completion are plausibly the broken Redis session cleanup, not authentication processing.

### This also proves the keep-alive change wasn't the issue

Your Rust log now explicitly sends:

```text
Connection: keep-alive
```

and the server still returns:

```text
InvalidToken
```

So we can permanently remove `Connection: close` from the suspect list.

Likewise, the outgoing request is structurally fine:

```text
GET /api/v1/auth/windows/windows-login HTTP/1.1
Host: localhost
Authorization: Negotiate TlRMTVNTUAAD...
User-Agent: rust9x-windows-auth/1.0
Accept: */*
Connection: keep-alive
```

Kestrel itself confirms:

```text
Request starting HTTP/1.1 GET ...
1 candidate(s) found
Endpoint ... WindowsLogin ... is valid
Request matched endpoint ...
```

That is extremely strong evidence that the HTTP layer of the Rust implementation is doing its job.

## Now the real suspect is the NTLM Type-3 generated by your SSPI usage

Your Rust implementation explicitly calls SSPI with:

```rust
ClientRequestFlags::CONNECTION | ClientRequestFlags::ALLOCATE_MEMORY
```

and passes:

```rust
.with_target_name(target_name)
.with_input(...)
.with_output(...)
```

for both the initial and challenge phases. fileciteturn11file0

The server's response is not saying "wrong password".

It's saying:

```text
InvalidToken
```

That is a very different failure.

The credentials can be perfectly valid while the authentication message itself is unacceptable to the server.

### The strongest thing to test now: target name / SPN

The client currently creates:

```text
HTTP/localhost
```

as the SSPI target name.

Your server's NTLM challenge contains:

```text
DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

as the target computer information.

So the authentication exchange is effectively:

```text
client service target:
    HTTP/localhost

server machine:
    DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

This deserves a very serious test.

I would run the exact same application against:

```text
https://DESKTOP-I4OV31Q:5001/api/v1/auth/windows/windows-login
```

and let the Rust client generate:

```text
HTTP/DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

as the SSPI target name.

The ideal identity alignment becomes:

```text
TCP              DESKTOP-I4OV31Q:5001
TLS SNI          DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
HTTP Host        DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
NTLM target      HTTP/DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
NTLM server      DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

instead of:

```text
TCP              localhost:5001
TLS SNI          localhost
HTTP Host        localhost
NTLM target      HTTP/localhost
NTLM server      DESKTOP-I4OV31Q
```

I would not assume this is definitely the bug, but after the server has explicitly reported `InvalidToken`, it is one of the first things I'd test.

### There is another particularly interesting detail

Your Type-3 token contains the expected username/domain material:

```text
rust9x
rsncra
```

and SSPI itself reports:

```text
InitializeSecurityContext (challenge) -> SUCCESS
Authenticate token generated (344 bytes)
```

That means the local Windows SSPI provider is happy with the credentials and challenge.

But **local success does not mean server acceptance**.

The server is using its own Windows Negotiate/SSPI implementation through ASP.NET Core, and that side is saying the token is invalid.

So I would focus on the exact inputs to `InitializeSecurityContext`, not on the NTLM wire encoding itself.

### One thing I'd investigate next in the Rust code

You have the same call structure for both stages:

```rust
.with_context_requirements(
    ClientRequestFlags::CONNECTION |
    ClientRequestFlags::ALLOCATE_MEMORY
)
.with_target_data_representation(DataRepresentation::Native)
.with_target_name(target_name)
```

fileciteturn11file0

I'd compare this against how a normal Windows HTTP authentication implementation drives SSPI.

Specifically, I would investigate whether the client should be requesting additional context properties or whether `CONNECTION` is the correct semantic for the HTTP Negotiate case used by ASP.NET Core.

The fact that the returned Type-3 token is exactly 344 bytes and has valid NTLMSSP structure means we're probably **one SSPI detail away**, rather than dealing with a completely broken NTLM implementation.

### And the server logs give us an excellent future breakpoint

You now have a clean distinction:

**Before this point:**

```text
Request matched endpoint
```

**Authentication failure:**

```text
Negotiate error code: ClientError
AuthenticationFailureException: InvalidToken
```

**After this point:**

```text
WindowsLogin()
```

is never reached.

So don't spend time modifying:

```csharp
WindowsAuthController.WindowsLogin()
WindowsAuthBusinessService
WindowsAuthSecurityHandler
```

yet. The Type-3 token is being rejected before all of that. The controller source confirms the action only starts after successful Windows authentication. fileciteturn5file0

### My current diagnosis

I'd put it roughly like this:

| Layer | Result |
|---|---|
| TCP | Working |
| TLS 1.2 | Working |
| HTTP request syntax | Working |
| Routing | Working |
| 401 challenge | Working |
| NTLM Type-2 extraction | Working |
| Local SSPI Type-3 generation | Working |
| Connection persistence | Working |
| ASP.NET Core Negotiate | **Rejects Type-3 as `InvalidToken`** |
| Controller | Never reached |

So the commit you showed earlier **did accomplish the HTTP handling part**. The remaining problem is no longer really an HTTP problem.

It is now a **Windows SSPI / NTLM interoperability problem between your Rust-generated Type-3 and ASP.NET Core's Negotiate handler**.

And the Redis outage should be fixed/disabled for testing too, because right now it is turning a clean authentication test into a 5–30 second mess. It isn't the cause of `InvalidToken`, but it's obscuring the timing and generating huge amounts of unrelated noise.

The next experiment I'd personally do is **use `DESKTOP-I4OV31Q` instead of `localhost` for the HTTP/TLS/SSPI target**, because your NTLM challenge and client target currently disagree on the service identity.
